STATUS: USER-REQUESTED DESIGN CONCEPT STUDY, READY FOR MANUAL CODEX AUTHORIZATION. BLENDER ONLY; NO LIVE WEBSITE IMPLEMENTATION OR AUTOMATIC NEXT STAGE.

GOAL
Design a completely new, culturally grounded Gullyverse environment based on Indian street hip-hop, but preserve the logic and recognizable visitor journey of the existing immersive Gullyverse project. Instead of cave → quarry → big stage, the new sequence is:
REAL INDIAN NEIGHBORHOOD LANE AT NIGHT → HAZE WITH DISTANT CROWD → LANE OPENS ONTO A ROOFTOP CYPHER → EXISTING NAVIGATION SIGNBOARD → TEAM (RIGHT) / EVENTS (LEFT) WALLS → STRAIGHT-AHEAD CROWD-CAMERA CUTSCENE → LARGE EXISTING DIGITAL SCREEN PLAYING THE GULLYVERSE AFTERMOVIE.
The world must be real, localized, slightly gritty, lived-in and musical, not a Western industrial wasteland, futuristic city, generic party venue or photorealistic reproduction of a named real Indian street. Design one strong spatial layout and show THREE ART-DIRECTION VARIANTS of that SAME layout.

AUTHORITATIVE USER SCENE DESCRIPTION — DO NOT SUBSTITUTE
1. Landing page starts facing down an authentic Indian residential/commercial neighborhood LANE AT NIGHT. BOTH left and right sides have small stores; some OPEN with natural warm interior light and subtle activity, some CLOSED with metal shutters. Show believable Indian painted/handmade signage, balconies, electrical cables, weathered plaster/concrete, posters, scooters, crates and varied but restrained street clutter. No caricature or random neon covering everything.
2. The lane ends ahead in distance haze; visitors see silhouettes and movement of a CROWD through haze. The crowd must be visible enough to create curiosity, with plausible lighting and sound implied in storyboard. Do not visually reveal the full screening area from the starting camera.
3. Walking to the end of the lane opens into the center of a ROOFTOP CYPHER gathering. Spatial feasibility matters: if the lane and cypher are at different levels, solve access with an architecturally credible short ramp, roof bridge, lane that is already on an elevated neighborhood level, or concealed rising transition. The user has not approved a staircase/elevator requirement; show and justify a clean accessible transition rather than arbitrarily forcing one. Keep the emotional reveal without a loading cut.
4. At the FIRST central hub point where the lane ends and the rooftop opens up, place the EXISTING Gullyverse NAVIGATION SIGNBOARD with its same landmark role and future interaction structure. It must be discoverable and readable, without obstructing sightlines or navigation.
5. If the visitor goes STRAIGHT AHEAD toward the crowd, trigger a cinematic short cutscene. Camera travels THROUGH or BETWEEN crowd silhouettes; the final reveal is the existing GULLYVERSE DIGITAL SCREEN. The crowd gathered to watch the AFTERMOVIE, not a band on a giant concert stage. Keep or conceptually reuse the existing video screen, remove old physical stage platform/truss/bars/lighting towers from the NEW CONCEPT ONLY. Don't erase existing production assets.
6. Team destination is to the visitor's RIGHT from the central hub: a wall covered with lead/OC photographs arranged like photocopied WANTED-style street posters, notices and paste-ups. This is a graphic design treatment, NOT a claim of real criminal wrongdoing. Use available authorized images; otherwise neutral clearly labeled placeholders, not invented real team faces.
7. Events destination is to the visitor's LEFT from the central hub: event/lineup posters pasted onto a believable exterior parapet/building wall. Use existing authorized real event art if accessible, or clearly labeled placeholders only. Both poster-wall destinations must retain space and camera positions for future click/hotspot interactions.
8. Integrate rooftop parapets, neighboring mid-rise buildings, terraces, soft spill lights, murals, possible cypher details (circle/turntables/breakdance space) and city atmosphere, but do not overcrowd the central visitor route. Favor believable India-specific contextual details and purposeful Gullyverse identity over generic graffiti/neon.

MASTER SPATIAL STUDY — ONE LAYOUT
Plan the zone relations with a scaled annotated overhead map:
Zone A — NIGHT LANE / LANDING SCREEN (shops to both sides).
Zone B — HAZE AND DISTANT-CROWD TRANSITION.
Zone C — ROOFTOP CYPHER HUB WITH ORIGINAL SIGNBOARD.
Zone D-RIGHT — TEAM POSTER WALL / lead/OC gallery.
Zone E-LEFT — EVENTS POSTER WALL / events gallery.
Zone F-STRAIGHT — CROWD WALK-THROUGH CUTSCENE.
Zone G — LARGE DIGITAL AFTERMOVIE SCREEN AND WATCHING CROWD.
Keep the existing conceptual destination system, navigation, map/fast travel, interactive poster links, single Canvas, Aftermovie playback, return/history flow and cutscene trigger system in mind. Do not claim exact source component compatibility or collision/performance equivalence until implementation is measured. Build a realistic layout where hubs and routes can eventually map to existing functionality with modest adaptation.

THREE BLENDER ART-DIRECTION VERSIONS (SAME OVERHEAD LAYOUT / ROUTE / CAMERA RIG)
A — GROUNDED INDIAN NEIGHBORHOOD: normal lived-in gully at night, restraint, convincing hand-painted shop identity, natural lighting, subtle festival takeover.
B — FESTIVAL-HEAVY BLOCK: same shop fronts, lane/rooftop geometry and destinations, but stronger festival wayfinding, screen glow, graphics, pasted posters, brand layering and visible crowd energy; do not make it cyberpunk.
C — BALANCED / RECOMMENDED: credible neighborhood intimacy transitioning into an iconic rooftop cypher/watch party, carefully placed street-art/gig elements and tasteful Gullyverse branding. This is the likely strongest default but do NOT select it without user comparison.
A/B/C must not silently use different routes or screen positions. Preserve identical named camera poses / lens / FOV / scene dimensions so they can be visually compared fairly; vary ART DIRECTION (lighting/props/graphics/density) and document changes, rather than presenting three unrelated places.

BLENDER-FIRST DELIVERABLES (NO RUNTIME CODE YET)
Create editable and properly organized .blend source scene(s) with named layers/collections for architecture, signage, shops, roof, crowd, screen, posters and camera rigs. Rough production-oriented mesh blockouts are sufficient but should be spatially coherent and visually understandable. For EACH A/B/C version render:
1. Actual LANDING PAGE HERO camera down the lane at night with crowd silhouettes through haze.

9. Top-down ORTHOGRAPHIC map and a simple circulation/flow overlay.
10. One narrow mobile landing-screen crop, showing readability/quality.
Provide equal-sized contact sheets per version and direct A/B/C matched-camera side-by-side comparisons, plus a short storyboard/animatic of the 15–30-second proposed crowd-reveal camera move if feasible (label as a design study, not runtime evidence). Include sensible height and roof transitions with section/elevation sketches where needed. Make the central signboard physically navigable; Team right, Events left, Screen straight.
Do not pass off AI-generated stills or reference photography as Blender scene proof. Include source/asset origin and usage license provenance; if missing exact existing props/screen files, create clean temporary PROXY objects with truthful labels and record what integration assets Codex must later retrieve.

1. PHYSICALLY CONNECT ALL ADJOINING HOUSES ALONG THE LEFT/RIGHT/FAR PERIMETER

2. PRESERVE THE APPROVED ARCHITECTURE

The user explicitly approves Rev12's multi-storey direction. All 11 connected residential buildings now have 3–5 real courtyard-facing storeys. Treat the architecture as locked.

Do not change the rooftop square dimensions, buildings, shared walls, roof heights, navigation, Aftermovie screen, Team/Events areas, street layout, approved graffiti or existing lighting. Keep the original 40-second straight walking route and its clearances.

3. IMPROVE THE CROWD'S HUMAN REALISM

The current 180-person Blender audience is visually unsatisfactory because the people resemble low-poly mannequins.

The user wants realistic-looking human spectators while preserving the original Blender C180 distribution.

Replace or substantially improve the visible character geometry using anatomically believable, performance-conscious human models.

Focus on natural human proportions, shoulders, necks, arms, hands, heads, facial structure, hair, legs, footwear and actual clothing volume.

Aim for grounded stylized realism suitable for an immersive 3D website—not exaggerated cartoon people or expensive photorealistic NPCs.

Do not reuse the previously rejected website Man.glb and Animated-Woman.glb models as the chosen solution.

4. CREATE BELIEVABLE AUDIENCE DIVERSITY

This is an Indian underground hip-hop gathering, not a formal conference audience.

Use contemporary urban streetwear: oversized T-shirts, hoodies, casual shirts, cargos, jeans, sneakers, caps and restrained accessories.

Introduce varied hairstyles, skin tones, body builds, silhouettes and natural adult heights.

Target at least 12 meaningfully different character silhouettes/outfit combinations and 8 natural pose families, with additional combinations and material variation.

Avoid identical blue-grey clothing, repetitive bodies and obvious adjacent clones. Keep the entire audience visually coherent with the grounded Indian-neighborhood atmosphere.

5. MAKE THE CROWD BEHAVE NATURALLY

The current audience contains too many similarly positioned raised arms and rigid poses.

Instead:
- Most spectators should stand naturally and watch the screen.
- Some can cheer, raise one arm or record with phones.
- Some should stand in small conversational groups or lean toward friends.
- Add natural weight shifts, relaxed elbows, asymmetric stances and subtle differences in head direction.
- Avoid synchronized or mechanically repeated poses.

If appropriate, create a separate optional subtle animation study: head movements, weight shifts, small gestures or cheering with independent timing.

Static visual quality is the priority. Do not begin website animation integration.

6. PRESERVE THE ORIGINAL 180-PERSON COMPOSITION

Retain exactly 180 actor anchors, with the same original XY footprints, screen-facing arrangement and foot grounding.

Preserve the filled central audience, roughly semicircular overall distribution, irregular scattered outer edges and sparse transition around the navigation board.

Do not recreate a mathematically perfect arc, empty center gap or new clear corridor through the audience.

Preserve the original approach route, screen visibility and pedestrian clearances.

Rev12 already contains the proposed +7% height version. Do not apply another 7% height increase. Keep the original, +6%, +7% and +9% comparison controls; treat final stature as still subject to visual review.

Record a per-actor mapping between the original crowd and the new character representation.

7. BUILD AND EVALUATE A PILOT FIRST

Before replacing all 180 characters, build an 18–24-person sample with representative audience positions.

Show matched actual Blender renders of old versus new characters under identical lighting and camera conditions.

Include close human views at approximately 2 m, 5 m and 10 m.

Assess whether the new audience genuinely looks more human. If people still resemble cylinders, blocks or rigid robots, correct the pilot before expanding.

Once the pilot meets the realism objective, extend the system to all 180 actors using modular models, shared materials and reusable geometry.

Use appropriately licensed or original meshes and textures. Record asset provenance.

8. PERFORMANCE AND VALIDATION

Avoid 180 unrelated high-poly character assets.

Prefer reusable rigs, shared materials, instancing or batching where appropriate, and a documented near/mid/far LOD strategy for later implementation.

Provide crowd-specific triangle counts, material counts, asset memory estimates and scene totals compared with the Rev12 314,826-triangle scene.

- Most people should look like attentive spectators (relaxed arms, weight on one leg, hands by sides/pockets, crossed arms, subtle head tilt).
